Most people haven’t heard of kratom. What is it, and who’s using it?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Theresa covers substance use and addiction and co-writes the Morning Rounds newsletter. You can reach Theresa on Signal at theresagaff.97.

Over the last few years, the kratom plant and supplements made from it have garnered increasing attention from public health leaders. This broad category of products, which largely skirts any regulation, has seen major surges in potency, while becoming omnipresent on smoke shop and gas station shelves. Health officials have warned that concentrated compounds like 7-hydroxymitragynine (7-OH) are an emerging public health threat, and federal policies to limit their availability are pending. In the meantime, multiple states and smaller localities have issued emergency bans on 7-OH, and in some cases, on all kratom products.

Despite the messaging and rising use, few Americans are familiar with kratom or the newer derivative products. They’re made with compounds that have scientific names like 7-OH, mitragynine pseudoindoxyl (MGPI) and dihydro-7-hydroxy mitragynine (MGM-15) but are often sold on shelves with names like Dozo Perks, Roxy, and UltraOhmz Pseudo Supreme. New survey data from the Annenberg Public Policy Center show about two-thirds (66%) of Americans have never heard of kratom. Among those who had at least some familiarity, few understood the difference between its natural leaf form and more synthetic products.

That confusion carries over to the associated risks. In the survey of nearly 2,000 people, 66% were unsure if kratom products posed a serious public safety hazard. And most people didn’t understand health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s stance on regulation — a question that’s become complicated by repeated reports of kratom industry lobbying influence on the DEA’s proposed action.

STAT spoke with researchers and people who use or have used kratom and 7-OH to get a full picture of what these products are, along with their potential risks and benefits.

To an untrained eye, a kratom leaf might resemble a bay leaf. It grows on evergreen trees indigenous to Southeast Asian countries, including Thailand, Malaysia, and Indonesia. For centuries, people there have chewed the leaf or brewed it into tea for its stimulant-like effects.

Kratom (pronounce it with a hard or soft “a,” your choice) contains dozens of alkaloids, but mitragynine is the most abundant and the best understood. It acts as a partial agonist that binds to mu-opioid receptors in the nervous system. (Partial agonists produce less of a response from the receptor than full agonists, such as fentanyl or oxycodone.)

The first kratom products to arrive in the U.S. were made by drying and grinding up those leaves to create a matcha-like powder often used as a tea or swallowed in a capsule. In low quantities, it acts as a mild stimulant that can appear similar to coffee. In higher doses, people have used kratom to stave off chronic pain and even to help ease withdrawal from opioids. (There are no evidence-based dose recommendations, and there’s a lot of variation in both product potency and personal preference.)

“We call it swamp water, so that should give you a good visual,” said Madalyn Sklar, who uses kratom to ease chronic pain caused by degenerative disc disease, and hosts two podcasts on kratom. The first time Sklar drank the bitter tea made from kratom powder, she added orange juice as a sweetener, but still “it was so gross that I almost puked.” She’s taken it nearly every day since 2019, and says the results are better than when she was on meds like OxyContin and gabapentin.

She takes two teaspoons of powder — which works out to about 70-75 milligrams of mitragynine — every six hours. For her, there’s no high or euphoria. But she can do simple tasks that she couldn’t before, such as walk her dog and bend down to pick up its poop.

Sklar hasn’t experienced withdrawal symptoms when she’s occasionally stopped taking kratom and returned to more traditional pain medication. Research is limited, but has found that up to around 38% of kratom users may develop use disorders. In studies, withdrawal from kratom has appeared mild on a scale that measures clinical opioid withdrawal, with symptoms like anxiety, fatigue, gastrointestinal issues, and restlessness.

Before semi-synthetic products came onto the scene, most public health concern around kratom was directed at extracts and tinctures, recalled Sklar. Many of these products still tout mitragynine as the active ingredient, but in much more concentrated volumes than the whole leaf powder — think 100 to 300 milligrams of mitragynine in a bottle of liquid the size of a 5-hour Energy. (There are currently no federal proposals that aim to regulate concentrated mitragynine products.)

“But people got really interested in the other alkaloids over time,” said Katherine Hill, an epidemiologist at the Yale School of Public Health who has studied kratom. “And specifically they got interested in 7-hydroxymitrogynine.”

The alkaloid 7-OH does not appear in the fresh kratom leaf that people in Southeast Asia chew, according to Hill. It shows up in the dried leaf powder products, in small amounts. It’s also a metabolite, meaning that when the body absorbs mitragynine, some of it turns it into 7-OH. Once inside the body, 7-OH, like mitragynine, is a partial agonist to the mu-opioid receptor.

The products made with 7-OH — including tablets, gummies, shots, and drink powders — are semi-synthetic; they contain artificially concentrated amounts of the compound. When the products first started popping up, they were often sold in tablets containing 12-14 milligrams, users said. Those can offer sedative effects and pain relief. But now, tablets containing 25, 50, and even 100 milligrams of 7-OH are available.

“The first time I heard of 7-OH, it was very negative,” said Twinkle VanFleet, who co-hosts a kratom podcast with Sklar.

While natural kratom products carry a risk of dependence and what’s thought to be mild withdrawal at low doses, there’s growing concern that concentrated 7-OH products are even more addictive, and that withdrawal can be as bad as coming off a traditional opioid. Hill is working on a study of 7-OH users that will look at both tolerance and withdrawal. But for now, the risks are mainly documented in case reports, series, and other anecdotal accounts.

VanFleet suffers from intense pain from an injury that led to complex regional pain syndrome, and she has a deep mistrust of the medical system. Kratom didn’t ease her symptoms, so she eventually tried 7-OH, and found comfort in what she calls “nibbles” of smaller tablets. She currently takes around 12 milligrams per day and worries about a ban by the Drug Enforcement Administration. Still, she shares concerns about the availability of higher-dose products.

“I do see both sides of a lot of this,” VanFleet said. “In my perfect world, there would be 3, 5, and 10 milligram tablets, period. If even that.”

Greg Kiesow had been sober from alcohol for nearly 20 years and retired for three days when he went into a smoke shop that was promoting 7-OH products. The shopkeeper had heard it was some sort of natural energy supplement, like coffee, and gave Kiesow a free sample of tablets. “I took it home, and I took it. And 10 to 15 minutes later, it wasn’t coffee. It was a very powerful opioid,” Kiesow said.

Kiesow said he quickly developed an addiction. After 10 months, which he described as an extremely isolated period spent sitting on his back porch watching the birds at his bird feeder, he began to taper himself off the products. A few weeks later, he formed Kratom Anonymous, which has 15 virtual meetings per week for people looking to quit.

As 7-OH gained popularity, newer synthetic products have continued to crop up, including some with concentrated levels of mitragynine pseudoindoxyl (MGPI), another metabolite of mitragynine. Over the summer, the DEA proposed a ban of MGPI at any quantity. But an updated proposal in October, pushed by a lobbyist for the American Kratom Association, would allow small amounts of the compound to show up in products. Instead of an outright ban, the DEA now proposes limiting both MGPI and 7-OH based on the ratio of those compounds to mitragynine in any given product.

The reason for the change, according to the DEA, is that trace amounts of MGPI could appear in natural kratom. But the safety of small amounts is unclear. Test results presented in recent wrongful death lawsuits suggest that a natural kratom powder product, used by somebody who died after taking it, was likely contaminated with MGPI.

As more people use kratom and other associated products, there’s a growing recognition of the potential risk for addiction. Over 10 years, the National Poison Data Center found a 1,200% increase in kratom-related exposure reports — from 258 in 2015 to 3,434 in 2025. That includes all types of products. Clinicians who run addiction treatment centers say, over the last year, they’ve seen a rise in patients seeking help to get off 7-OH in particular.

But data on the most serious risks — overdose and death — are murky. The poison center data showed 233 deaths over the decade, most of which involved other substances like opioids, benzodiazepines, stimulants, and alcohol. (Researchers believe kratom can exacerbate the danger of a drug like fentanyl, and even more common medications like certain antidepressants.) A 2023 investigation by the Tampa Bay Times found that, over nearly 10 years, more than 580 people in Florida alone died from overdoses that involved kratom products.

Because both 7-OH and MGPI are metabolites of mitragynine, it can be difficult to draw conclusions about a given death, even with toxicology data. Complicating the matter even further: When tested, many kratom products — natural and synthetic — are found to have different concentration levels than the labels advertise, and some have been found to contain compounds that were not on the label at all.

Sklar and VanFleet are both proponents of regulations, not bans, on all types of kratom-related products. Others who advocate for natural kratom are working hard to distance themselves from the more synthetic products.

“Any who unwittingly support every concentrated 7-OH product are not defending the natural kratom plant. It may be helping to destroy it,” Mac Haddow, the American Kratom Association’s lobbyist, said in a recent video. “The solution is not to defend everything. The solution is to draw a clear, bright line.”

But others said they’ve suffered from taking natural kratom products, too. Danelle Kawasaki first tried kratom at a dry bar in her Montana town. It came at room temperature, and was advertised as a natural, herbal tea. The concoction eased her anxiety, making her feel both calm and energetic, able to speak with anyone she wanted. At first it was a weekly treat, then a few times a week. After a few months, she was having two drinks per day. She’d wake up with a jolt of anxiety, which she attributes to the tea not being in her system overnight. Without more, she’d be sick, crawling to the bathroom to puke.

It frustrates Kawasaki when people try to tell her that 7-OH, not natural kratom, is the problem. “This argument comes up all the time and it drives me nuts,” she said. “I know what was going on — it was the actual kratom tea powder. It wasn’t a concentrated form.”

At one point, she was drinking nearly 140 ounces of the tea per day. When she finally stopped, cold turkey, the withdrawal was its own dayslong nightmare.

Now, she advocates that the Fort Peck tribe, of which she’s a member, ban kratom on the reservation. For Kawasaki, “the risks outweigh the remedy that people are searching for.”

STAT’s coverage of chronic health issues is supported by a grant from Bloomberg Philanthropies. Our financial supporters are not involved in any decisions about our journalism.

Theresa covers substance use and addiction and co-writes the Morning Rounds newsletter. You can reach Theresa on Signal at theresagaff.97.

Understand how science, health policy, and medicine shape the world every day

By Annemarie G. Hirsch and Jennifer Silva

Reporting from the frontiers of health and medicine

Opinion: AI depends on medical records that don’t always tell the full truth about women

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

By Annemarie G. Hirsch and Jennifer Silva

Hirsch and Silva are authors of “Seen But Not Heard: What Medical Records Don’t Tell Us About Women’s Lives.”

In September, the Gallup-Pivotal Women’s Health Survey reported that 34% of U.S. women strongly or somewhat agreed that a health care provider minimized or dismissed their symptoms in the past five years. Thirty-three percent reported being misdiagnosed or unable to get a diagnosis, compared with 21% of men reporting these experiences.

Medical records tell a different story. An epidemiologist and a sociologist, we recently released a study where we paired electronic medical records with detailed interviews with 87 women. We talked to these women about their pain and illnesses, work, family, poverty, violence, and encounters with the health care system.

Fifty-three of the women described a trust-breaking encounter with a health system, but only seven of them had medical records that documented mistrust. Thirty-two women reported experiencing a misdiagnosis, but misdiagnosis was only documented for eight of these women. Thirty women reported that they felt that clinicians treated them with disrespect, but this was not documented in the records anywhere.

For more than a decade, Kate lived with debilitating pelvic pain. Her doctors repeatedly attributed her symptoms to obesity. Her medical record reflected these assumptions.

When Kate underwent bariatric surgery and lost a significant amount of weight, her clinical notes became optimistic.

However, her symptoms persisted. Medical records pivoted from linking symptoms from obesity to mental health factors.

“Gastrointestinal malfunctions arising from mental factors.”

The turning point came only after Kate attended an appointment with her new husband. In the clinical notes, the provider documented Kate’s husband’s concerns:

For the first time in 10 years, doctors conducted the exams needed to finally diagnose Kate with endometriosis.

Kate’s medical record accurately documented years of conversations about weight and mental health. What it failed to capture was equally important: the cost of years spent with an undiagnosed disease, the frustration of not being believed, and the erosion of trust that occurs when symptoms are repeatedly explained away rather than investigated. Kate told us, “If I was tired, it was because I was overweight. If I had cramps, it was because I was overweight. Migraines, I was overweight. … They’re not hearing what you’re saying, and that compounded the issues that were going on.”

We often think of medical records as objective accounts of a patient’s health. They are not. Medical records are narratives, shaped by the questions clinicians ask and the assumptions they bring into the room. Medical record data reflect clinical decisions as well as biology. The language clinicians use and the details they choose to record can reinforce stereotypes that follow patients from one encounter to the next, influencing how future clinicians interpret symptoms, assess credibility, and make treatment decisions.

Medical record data also reflect what patients feel comfortable disclosing. What women share with their doctors depends on how much they trust them and how safe they feel. When people fear judgment, dismissal, or not being believed, important parts of their stories may remain unspoken, and thus, undocumented.

This fear may why 53 women in our data described a trust-breaking clinical encounter in their interview, while only seven of their medical records noted their mistrust. This is particularly a problem for women, who feel the pressure to be pleasant and pliant with doctors for fear of not being taken seriously.

Across the country, clinics and hospitals are adopting AI scribes that transcribe recorded interactions between doctor and patient and produce a clinical note. AI tools can be used to search and synthesize medical records to help with diagnostics and treatment plans, some with great success.

However, these tools are only as good as the data in the medical record. If a woman’s symptoms are filtered through assumptions about her weight or mental health, and the true story of her pain is omitted, AI may learn and reproduce how the woman is framed in the record.

For AI to achieve its full potential in women’s health, medical records need to tell the complete story. Health care settings must be safe spaces where women can tell their stories; clinicians need to listen to women, even when their experiences do not fit clinical expectations; and clinicians need to document in the medical record when diagnoses are delayed or incorrect, without fear of being penalized.

Armed with these data, AI has the potential to do more than replicate biases of the past. AI could help identify patterns of missed diagnoses, reduce disparities in care, and ensure that more women are heard.

Annemarie G. Hirsch, Ph.D., M.P.H., and Jennifer Silva, Ph.D., are authors of “Seen But Not Heard: What Medical Records Don’t Tell Us About Women’s Lives.”

STAT+: After Medicare punts RPM overhaul to 2029, advocates see window to fight back

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Mario covers technology in health care, including FDA regulation of artificial intelligence; how Medicare pays for health tech; the use of AI in clinical care; mental health chatbots; and consumer wearables. He’s also the co-author of the free, twice weekly STAT Health Tech newsletter. You can reach Mario on Signal at mariojoze.13.

Medicare regulators this week pushed forward with a controversial plan to ban hospitals and other providers from using third parties to deliver remote patient monitoring services, despite overwhelming opposition from health tech companies, hospitals, insurers, and specialty organizations.

But regulators also offered providers a reprieve in the form of a two-year delay in implementation that’s aimed at allowing time for providers to build in-house services. Advocates representing vendors, hospitals, and other stakeholders see that window as an opportunity to push for another plan.

“The story is that we have two years to come up with something better,” said Christopher Adamec, a lobbyist who advises the Remote Monitoring Leadership Council, a group of leading vendors. “My mission here is to find and move forward a value-based payment pathway or some set of guardrails, or some alternative to this.”

Guy Roux, it’s over

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In four and a half decades of (almost) absolute loyalty at the head of AJ Auxerre, Guy Roux has set almost unbreakable longevity records in the landscape of French football. The iconic coach with the hat has notably etched his name in history by directing 894 first division matches (including four during his very short stint in Lens), an absolute peak in Ligue 1 which testifies to a bygone era when projects were long-term.

This statistical prowess is not limited to the national championship, since the child from Colmar has long stood alone on another summit: that of European victories acquired at the head of a French club. A historic record that modern football and its binge on matches are about to bring down

It is precisely this record of victories on the continental scene which is today in the process of escaping him. By winning on the pitch at Bayer Leverkusen with Olympique de Marseille, Bruno Genesio achieved the 41st success of his career in the European Cup, in all competitions, with a French club. A major performance which allows him to match the legendary mark of the former Auxerre coach.

Having increased his record from OL to LOSC, then from Rennes to OM, Bruno Genesio is preparing to overtake Guy Roux, but could quickly have to lose to Luis Enrique, who already has 31 victories at the head of PSG in just over two seasons.

If Bruno Genesio has to his credit the semi-final of the Champions League played with Olympique Lyonnais in 2020 or the direct qualification for the round of 16 with Losc, two years ago, Guy Roux’s continental achievements with a club with the profile of Auxerre are a romantic feat.

With incomparable financial means, the Burgundian technician took AJA to the semi-final of the UEFA Cup in 1993 – cruelly eliminated on penalties by Borussia Dortmund – before inviting himself among the eight best teams on the continent in the quarter-final of the Champions League in 1997, defeating European giants on the Abbé-Deschamps lawn.

During his long successive leases at AS Monaco and especially on the Arsenal bench, the Alsatian technician totaled more than 120 victories on the European scene (including the Champions League and the various C3s), placing him irremediably out of reach of most French technicians.

Si son âge de 33 ans pourrait freiner Zinédine Zidane, son apport chiffré reste notable.

Ces choix en attaque s'inscrivent dans le cadre des deux dernières journées de la phase de poules de la Ligue des nations de l'UEFA 2026-2027. Dans le groupe A1 de la Ligue A, la France est en tête avec 10 points devant l'Italie qui compte 7 points. L'équipe de France se déplacera pour affronter l'Italie le jeudi 12 novembre 2026 à 20h45 à Milan, à San Siro. Les Bleus recevront ensuite la Turquie le dimanche 15 novembre 2026 à 20h45 à Bordeaux, au Matmut Atlantique, deux rencontres qui seront retransmises en direct sur TF1.

Freiburg’s Jordy Makengo on marking Mohamed Salah: “I always used to play as him on PlayStation!”

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

SC Freiburg defender Jordy Makengo enjoyed a rather unusual encounter during Thursday’s 1-0 Conference League victory over Trabzonspor. The 25-year-old left-back found himself competing against former Liverpool superstar Mohamed Salah, a player he had previously spent considerable time controlling on his PlayStation.

Makengo played an important role in Freiburg’s impressive away victory, helping Julian Schuster’s side contain the Egyptian international as the Bundesliga representatives secured their third consecutive competitive win. As detailed in Get German Football News‘ match report and player ratings, Salah became increasingly dangerous as the match progressed, forcing goalkeeper Mio Backhaus into a crucial intervention in the 61st minute.

Freiburg’s defensive unit largely succeeded in limiting the former Liverpool star’s influence. Makengo, supported by Vincenzo Grifo and Philipp Lienhart, played a particularly important role in closing down the spaces available to Salah on Trabzonspor’s right flank.

Speaking in the mixed zone following the victory, Makengo revealed just how extraordinary he found the experience of lining up against one of global football’s biggest stars.

“I’m a big FIFA gamer on PlayStation,” Makengo said. “I always used to play as him, and now I’m playing against him in real life. That’s simply incredible, and I’m really happy about it.”

Freiburg head coach Julian Schuster, apparently less enthusiastic about video games than his young defender, nevertheless saw the amusing side of the situation.

“If the PlayStation has something positive to offer, perhaps this is it,” Schuster joked.

The lighthearted exchange followed what Schuster described as Freiburg’s best performance of the season. The Sport-Club’s aggressive first-half pressing and disciplined defensive work after the restart enabled the Bundesliga side to overcome a Trabzonspor team that had won seven consecutive matches.

Beyond the amusing PlayStation anecdote, Thursday’s performance represented another encouraging milestone in Makengo’s development. The left-back endured a difficult 2025/26 campaign, struggling to establish himself consistently and making several costly defensive mistakes. As examined in GGFN‘s extensive Freiburg preseason tactical preview, Makengo entered the summer facing considerable competition from long-serving captain Christian Günter and returning youngster Berkay Yilmaz.

The prospect of losing further ground in Freiburg’s defensive hierarchy appeared entirely plausible. Instead, Makengo responded with an excellent preseason, forcing Schuster to reconsider the established pecking order. His improvement has also been evident in his attacking contributions. As previously noted in GGFN‘s coverage of Freiburg’s record Bundesliga start, Makengo supplied four assists in five appearances earlier this season after failing to register a single goal contribution in his previous 58 matches.

The defender also made his senior international debut for DR Congo during the October international break, adding another significant achievement to his increasingly encouraging campaign. Makengo’s attacking confidence was again visible during Freiburg’s recent Bundesliga victory over Schalke 04, when the left-back combined effectively with Derry Scherhant to repeatedly threaten the visitors down Freiburg’s left flank.

Thursday’s encounter in Trabzon, however, demanded a different set of qualities.

Facing a player of Salah’s experience, technical ability, and attacking intelligence required Makengo to exercise considerable defensive discipline. The former Liverpool attacker did occasionally find a way through, particularly during Trabzonspor’s stronger second-half spell, but Freiburg’s collective defensive effort ultimately prevailed.

Makengo earned a 7/10 rating from GGFN for his contribution to the clean sheet.

For a player whose future at Freiburg had appeared uncertain not so long ago, the chance to test himself against one of the Premier League’s greatest modern attacking talents represented another important step forward.

And for Makengo, there was the added satisfaction of discovering what it feels like to face a familiar PlayStation favourite in real life.

Bitcoin Falls Below $76,000 as Chip Stocks Crash and Liquidations Hit $1 Billion: Why Is Crypto Down Today?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Why is crypto down today? A chip-stock crash sent Bitcoin below $76K, wiped out $1B in longs and pulled $430M from Bitcoin ETFs.

Short answer to “why is crypto down today?” Bitcoin fell 6% to below $76,000 after a Wall Street chip-stock crash on Thursday, November 5, wiped out $1.03 billion in leveraged bets and pulled $430 million out of Bitcoin ETFs. The selling hasn’t stopped overnight.

Bitcoin traded at about $75,700 on Binance on Friday morning, down from $80,547 at the start of Thursday. Ethereum lost 8.2% to $2,218, XRP fell 9.7% to $1.18 and Solana dropped 6.9% to $89.22. The Crypto Fear & Greed Index fell to 28, in “Fear”, from a neutral 50 a day earlier.

The trigger came from stocks. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index closed down 12.2%, its members Nvidia and AMD lost 9.3% and 17.3%, and the Nasdaq fell 5.5%. Crypto stocks went with them: Strategy dropped 9.3% and Coinbase 9.7%.

So what turned a tech sell-off into a crypto crash, and where can Bitcoin find a floor?

Today was a 4σ day for equities. The S&P 500 fell 4.4% and the Nasdaq fell 5.5%, a decline seen roughly once every 19 months. Bitcoin fell about 5%. $STRC closed at $99.56, just 44 cents below par.

— Michael Saylor (@saylor) November 5, 2026

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best Low-Cap Crypto

Wall Street opened at 14:30 UTC, and Bitcoin broke within the hour. The 15:00 UTC candle on Binance fell from $78,288 to $75,568, the day’s busiest hour with $513 million traded.

Yahoo Finance’s market coverage linked the stock rout to the Democrats’ clear midterm win and what it could mean for AI infrastructure spending and its regulation. Chipmakers, the biggest winners of the AI trade, took the hardest hit.

Leverage did the rest. CoinGlass counted $1.03 billion in liquidations over 24 hours, positions closed by force when margin ran out. Longs made up $924.94 million of that, or 89.4%, across 199,365 traders.

Bitcoin longs lost $255.88 million and Ether longs $163.83 million. The biggest single wipeout was a $10.94 million ETHUSDT position on Binance.

DISCOVER: Trump Media Moves $344 Million in Bitcoin to Crypto.com as TRUMP Hits a Record Low

US spot Bitcoin ETFs saw $430.2 million in net outflows on Thursday, and not one of the 12 funds took in money. SoSoValue’s ETF tracker shows Fidelity’s FBTC led with $199.87 million out, ahead of BlackRock’s IBIT at $104.97 million.

Grayscale’s GBTC lost $49.90 million, Franklin Templeton’s EZBC $32.92 million, VanEck’s HODL $23.64 million and ARKB $18.90 million.

It undid Wednesday in one session. Funds had added $74.6 million on November 4, the only inflow day so far this month. November’s first four trading days now add up to $869.8 million in outflows, on top of $515.9 million that left in October.

Fidelity’s turn is the detail to watch. FBTC took in $236.7 million on Wednesday while IBIT bled, then gave back most of it a day later.

Bitcoin closed Thursday at $76,088, just under its 200-day EMA at $76,536. That average had held as support through the October pullback.

The 50-day EMA at $79,822 now sits above the price, and the $81,971 high from before last week’s Binance hack is the next resistance beyond it.

On the downside, the $74,968 swing low is the first support. Below it, the 50% Fibonacci retracement of the July-to-September rally sits at $72,598.

One caveat: funding rates on Bitcoin perpetuals are still slightly positive on OKX, so longs haven’t fully cleared out even after a billion-dollar flush.

For anyone still asking “why is crypto down today?”, the answer runs through Nasdaq. If chip stocks steady when New York opens on Friday, Bitcoin has a shot at reclaiming its 200-day average. If they don’t, $74,968 is the line between a bad day and a deeper correction.

Bitcoin ETF Outflows Hit $430M as Hong Kong Expands Its BTC Market

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Bitcoin ETF outflows reached $430.2 million at the end of Thursday, as institutional investors continued withdrawing capital from US-listed spot products. Meanwhile, Hong Kong is expanding its crypto investment market, with four spot Bitcoin ETFs now available to investors.

Hong Kong’s private banking and wealth management assets reached HK$12.9 trillion ($1.65 trillion) in 2025, marking a 24% annual increase. Net fund inflows also climbed 79%, according to the latest Hong Kong Private Wealth Management Report 2026.

The contrasting developments put Hong Kong’s growing digital asset industry in focus. While American investors reduce exposure through listed products, Asian financial institutions are building new ways to access Bitcoin.

US spot Bitcoin ETF products recorded $430.2 million in net outflows on November 5. Fidelity’s FBTC led withdrawals with $199.9 million, followed by BlackRock’s IBIT at $105 million. Grayscale’s GBTC recorded another $49.9 million in withdrawals, while Franklin Templeton’s EZBC lost $32.9 million.

The latest session pushed November’s cumulative Bitcoin ETFs outflows to $869.8 million across four trading sessions. Net withdrawals across the latest seven finalized sessions reached approximately $1.36 billion.

That pressure follows a brief recovery on November 4, when the funds attracted $74.6 million. However, the latest withdrawals suggest institutional demand remains fragile.

The selling also comes as Bitcoin struggles to regain momentum. Investors are now watching if continued redemptions translate into further pressure on the underlying market.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

Hong Kong already operates a dedicated Bitcoin ETF market, rather than simply preparing to introduce cryptocurrency investment products.

The city has four spot Bitcoin ETFs listed on its exchange:

Hong Kong also offers two futures-based products: the CSOP Bitcoin Futures ETF and Samsung Bitcoin Futures Active ETF.

The first three spot products launched on April 30, 2024, making Hong Kong an early Asian market for regulated Bitcoin investment products. MicroBit subsequently joined the market in August 2025.

However, Hong Kong’s Bitcoin ETF market is not immune to withdrawals. November month-to-date flow figures from a regional ETF tracker showed outflows of $5.71 million from ChinaAMC, $1.58 million from Bosera HashKey and $68,880 from Harvest.

These figures show that institutional appetite remains uneven across both markets. Hong Kong’s smaller ETF market has not yet demonstrated that it can absorb the selling pressure seen in the United States.

Nevertheless, the wider financial industry continues building its digital asset exposure.

According to the Hong Kong Private Wealth Management Report 2026, 43% of surveyed firms have added cryptocurrency ETFs or exchange-traded products to their offerings. Another 25% are developing digital asset trading platforms, custody services or product ranges, and a further 28% plan to invest in digital assets within the next two to three years.

Hong Kong’s wider asset management industry reached HK$42.2 trillion, according to the Securities and Futures Commission. This gives traditional financial institutions a substantial pool of capital from which to develop new investment products.

The longer-term opportunity extends beyond immediate Bitcoin ETF subscriptions. Hong Kong is developing an institutional ecosystem that includes regulated products, custody services, and established wealth management firms.

The latest US withdrawals show that institutional demand can reverse quickly. However, Hong Kong’s expanding infrastructure displays that the competition for digital asset capital across global financial centers is far from over.

6 Mysteries Set on Generation Ships

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There’s been a few recent mysteries that take place aboard generation ships, and I love to spot a trend, so I figured I’d assemble them all for you here. If you think about it, a generation ship is the ultimate setting for locked-room mysteries and slow-burn thrillers: you can’t leave, you can’t turn around, nothing works like it used to, social hierarchies are calcifying into caste, and bitter disputes last decades. Doubts in the mission—or any possibility of achieving it—increase commensurate to the growing distance from the home planet, and any mistakes made by those in charge, or crimes committed by those in their orbit, are bound to be covered up in the name of unity. Truly, a fertile ground for crime fiction. For as long as the environmental systems are functioning.

In Flotilla, 17 ships remain of a generations-long search for a habitable planet, each holding upwards of a million residents, distrustful of outsiders and tired of their mission after hundreds of years in space. Three of the original vessels are gone from the fleet; two from catastrophic engineering failures, and another that simply…vanished. Hine’s heroine (appropriately named Carina, after a constellation) works as a watcher, scanning for dangers that aging sensors may have missed. She spends the long hours of her shift hoping to catch a glimpse of the missing ship and solve the mystery of its disappearance, while lusting for her coworker and covering up secrets of her own. She also occasionally zooms in on people dancing with themselves or arguing, à la Rear Window. Or perhaps, Rear Porthole? Anyway, you’ll have to wait till next July to dig into this excellent debut, but I’d advise getting on the library waitlist now.

Braking Day takes place on a generation ship whose colonists have rejected the home world’s dependence on AI in favor of neural augments and other bioware. At the novel’s start, an apprentice engineer begins to see visions of a floating girl screaming for help, and enlists his hacker cousin (the whole family has ties to the ship’s thriving underworld economy) to figure out if he’s hallucinating. Instead of a sign of oxygen deprivation of space-induced cabin sickness, she’s real: and they are not ready for the wild truths behind her sudden appearance.

This one takes place in the same world as Braking Day and functions well as a prequel. Line of Descent asks, what could motivate someone to go so far as to kill amidst the high stakes of disrupting order on a colony ship? Readers might end up surprised, but they will certainly feel satisfied.

Mahmud El Sayed, The Republic of Memory (Saga)

It is a universal truth that any functionary on a spaceship ship sufficiently well-placed will be susceptible to bribery, especially in a ship indulgently outfitted and gloriously inspired by the Ottoman Empire. In The Republic of Memory, gleefully corrupt officials, clever bootleggers, piratical smugglers, and nascent revolutionaries populate a generation ship (the second on this list) just over halfway to its destination, and beginning to crack at the seams. When a seemingly preventable disaster sends the ship’s factions into disarray, a former fighter gets assigned to investigate the accident as sabotage, recruiting plenty of unlikely allies on the way to the even more unlikely truth.

Peter F. Hamilton, A Hole in the Sky (Angry Robot)

This one’s experiencing the worst problem to possibly have in space (and one reminiscent of Spaceballs): the ship’s precious atmosphere is slowly but surely leaking away, at a rate that will guarantee suffocation long before the adventurous voyagers reach their planned destination. Can the ship’s ingenious crew fix their home among the stars? Will the answer to their conundrum be found with the rebellious elders of their community, those who refuse to commit suicide at the age of 65? While some on this list warn against seeking longevity, this novel does the opposite, reminding us of the social Darwinist impulses behind sacrificing the elderly or infirm in favor of the young and healthy.

Tade Thompson, Far From The Light of Heaven (Orbit)

Tade Thompson, known for the Rosewater trilogy and The Murders of Molly Southbourne, is quite possibly the most creative science fiction author around today, and Far From the Light of Heaven cements his reputation for stunning insights into the future and the human mind. In the distant future, a colonization ship docks in the Lagos sector, but some of the sleepers on board never awake, and an outside investigator is called in to find out what—or who—can be deemed responsible for the deaths.

Molly Odintz is the managing editor for CrimeReads and the editor of Austin Noir, now available from Akashic Books. She grew up in Austin and worked as a bookseller before becoming a Very Professional Internet Person. She lives in central Texas with her cat, Fritz Lang.

Qatar’s Tourism Recovery is Coming by Road, Not Air

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: Qatar markets.  Qatar Airways

Qatar’s visitor numbers are recovering on road traffic across the Saudi border, while air arrivals keep falling. Drive-in visitors tend to make shorter trips, which limits how much they can do for hotels.

Qatar’s tourism recovery from the U.S.-Iran war is running on road traffic across the Saudi border, a Skift analysis of Qatar Tourism data shows. .

International visitor arrivals in October totaled 336,000, down 10% from the same time last year, according to Qatar Tourism’s monthly arrivals data. September’s arrivals were up 1%. Both are a sharp turnaround from March, when arrivals fell 82% year over year to 63,000.

Qatar received 2.95 million visitors in the first 10 months of 2026, down 24% from 3.9 million in the same period last year.

Road is the only part of Qatar’s arrivals that is growing. Land arrivals, which all come through Qatar’s sole land border with Saudi Arabia, rose 18% year over year in October. Air arrivals fell 19%, registering an eighth straight month of decline.

That matters for hotels and for tourism revenue. Visitors flying in from long-haul markets typically stay longer and spend more than weekend drive-in gu

Tags: borders, hotels, qatar, saudi arabia, tourism

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Qatar's tourism recovery from the U.S.-Iran war is being carried by road traffic from Saudi Arabia, a Skift analysis of Qatar Tourism data shows. International arrivals reached 336,000 in October, down 10% year over year, and 2.95 million in the first 10 months of 2026, down 24% from 3.9 million a year earlier. Land arrivals through Qatar's only land border rose 18% in October and now make up 31% of all arrivals, up from 24% a year earlier. Air arrivals fell 19%, an eighth straight monthly decline, after collapsing 90% in March. Shaza Doha general manager Siddhartha Chowdhry said several pre-war source markets have yet to return, citing travel advisories, reduced airline seat capacity, higher travel insurance costs, and weaker traveler confidence. Hotel occupancy in September was 61.5%, down three percentage points year over year but well above April's 44.5% low, with RevPAR down 4%. Sea arrivals have been near zero since March, and the new cruise season is a test of whether ships return. Qatar is again leaning on events — MotoGP in early November and F1 from November 27 to 29 — after last year's F1 weekend drove ADR of about $241 and roughly 92% occupancy on its opening Friday. ValuStrat's Saad Lodhi expects cooler weather and F1 to lift performance but warned against counting on a repeat of last year's peaks.

StockFi: Connecting U.S. Stock with the On-Chain Ecosystem to Reshape Asset Circulation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Start with one stock. Connect two capital worlds.

6 November — Singapore — StockFi is advancing the connection between traditional equities and the on-chain ecosystem through U.S. stock tokenization. Built on BSC, the platform brings stock tokens and the meme ecosystem into its development strategy, exploring new ways to serve on-chain users worldwide through asset issuance, on-chain circulation and product applications.

StockFi’s approach connects the underlying value of equities, the utility of on-chain products and the reach of communities. Starting with its first stock offering, the platform plans to progressively develop asset onboarding, token issuance, trading environments and user access, giving equities broader reach and utility once they enter the on-chain market.

“Start with one stock. Connect two capital worlds.” This brand statement reflects StockFi’s understanding of asset tokenization: begin with a specific asset, connect markets through products, and support ecosystem development through sustained use.

Equities connect business operations with capital markets, reflecting investors’ assessments of industry development, commercial innovation and long-term value. On-chain networks offer a new digital environment in which assets can connect with users and products across regions through wallets, trading protocols and application interfaces.

When equities enter the on-chain market as tokens, the change extends beyond how assets are represented. It also affects how they are discovered, how they circulate, and how they connect with other digital assets and applications.

StockFi will focus its development on these areas.

Within the platform’s roadmap, equities and on-chain products require clearly defined structural connections. Products and users need convenient access points, while assets entering the market require appropriate liquidity and application support. Bringing these elements together is essential to delivering a complete asset tokenization experience.

Through ongoing product development, StockFi aims to combine the market familiarity of traditional equities with the open connectivity of the on-chain ecosystem, creating new opportunities for collaboration among asset issuers, ecosystem partners and eligible users.

StockFi’s product framework encompasses real stocks, stock tokens and MEME tokens. Each serves a distinct role in connecting assets with communities.

Real stocks form the underlying asset foundation, connecting company operations with traditional capital markets. Stock tokens provide the corresponding on-chain representation, serving as a product vehicle for trading, asset information and future applications. MEME tokens connect community culture, content distribution and user participation, helping the platform broaden its market reach.

This structure allows StockFi to develop both the asset and user sides of the platform.

On the asset side, key questions include where products originate, how they are issued and what rights holders receive. On the user side, participants need to understand a product’s purpose, find an access point and build familiarity through actual use. StockFi plans to bring these two sides closer together through an integrated platform experience.

The asset characteristics, uses and holder rights of stock tokens and MEME tokens will be explained separately. Their connections will take shape through trading environments, product integration and community participation, providing complementary foundations for platform development.

Communities are an important part of on-chain markets. Content, discussion and creative work built around shared themes help projects reach across regions and languages to establish distinctive brand cultures.

StockFi views the meme ecosystem as an important gateway to users and aims to bring community reach into the stock token experience.

Under its development plans, StockFi will select eligible projects from a pool of candidate MEME projects, work toward establishing trading pairs with stock tokens, and provide educational content and product introductions around specific assets. This approach can introduce community users to stock tokens while helping stock tokens attract broader attention and potential demand.

These connections require ongoing support from both content and products. After discovering an asset theme, users should be able to learn more about its background, structure and purpose. Once they access the product, they should also find clear information and operating instructions.

StockFi will therefore develop its community alongside its products. Through accurate asset introductions, accessible educational content and ongoing ecosystem engagement, the platform aims to turn brand awareness into product understanding and establish a foundation for long-term use.

StockFi sees asset issuance as the starting point of its platform services, with subsequent circulation and applications remaining central to its ongoing development.

For stock tokens, the platform plans to progressively connect asset arrangements, issuance services, on-chain trading and DApp access. These capabilities will be introduced in stages according to product delivery and partnership progress, creating a continuous path from asset information to practical use.

At the asset onboarding stage, the platform plans to work with relevant service providers on stock sourcing, custody and issuance structures, while clearly defining product information and holder rights.

For on-chain circulation, StockFi plans to develop stock token trading environments and connect wallets and liquidity services, providing product support for assets entering the on-chain market.

For the user experience, the platform will integrate asset displays, project materials and feature access through its DApp, helping users learn about assets, review information and access available services within a single product environment.

Together, these elements define StockFi’s product direction: make assets identifiable, information understandable and services usable. As its capabilities develop, the platform will further explore how stock tokens can be used across different on-chain applications.

Bringing equities into the on-chain market requires expertise from multiple fields. Asset services, technical development, wallet integration, liquidity and user education each play an essential role along the product journey.

StockFi will expand ecosystem partnerships around specific product needs, enabling partners to collaborate within clearly defined service scopes and delivery requirements.

For asset and issuance service partners, priorities will include asset selection, asset arrangements, custody, issuance and ongoing disclosure, establishing a clear product foundation for stock tokens.

For technology, wallet and liquidity partners, the platform will explore asset displays, trading access and application integrations to progressively improve how stock tokens are accessed and used.

For media and community partners, StockFi will provide consistent brand introductions, project materials and educational content, helping more users understand stock tokenization and the platform’s direction through ongoing communication.

StockFi aims to build an ecosystem in which every partnership serves a specific product need and every asset onboarded receives appropriate application support. As its network of partners and product use cases grows, the platform will progressively expand its ability to connect assets with users.

StockFi plans to begin with its first stock offering, connecting assets, issuance, circulation and user access, then refining the product journey based on actual operation.

The first offering will establish a reusable foundation for future development. Practical questions—how asset information is presented, how stock tokens enter trading environments, and how users understand and use products—will inform the platform’s subsequent expansion.

Building on this foundation, StockFi will consider product delivery, market demand and partnership progress as it explores onboarding additional stock tokens and eligible MEME projects, broadening its asset coverage and application range.

The platform’s long-term goal is to establish lasting connections among asset supply, product use and community participation: more products give users greater choice, more use cases support asset demand, and broader community reach contributes to ecosystem development.

Starting with one stock, StockFi aims to establish a product pathway into the on-chain market, creating a new connection between traditional equities and the global on-chain ecosystem.

Start with one stock. Connect two capital worlds.

StockFi is a U.S. stock tokenization platform built on BSC, connecting stock tokens with the meme ecosystem. The platform is developing stock token issuance, on-chain trading and applications. Through product services, community education and ecosystem partnerships, StockFi explores lasting connections between traditional equities and on-chain users worldwide, working toward a growth ecosystem in which assets, applications and communities develop together.

5 Infamous Women Killers and the Books You Should Read About Them

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

True Crime master and #1 New York Times bestselling author Gregg Olsen compiled his list of deadly women and the books written about them.

Read: Ordinary Wife: America’s Forgotten Serial Killer by Gregg Olsen and Morgan Olsen

Fittingly, the defense rested on Halloween 1921 in the case of arsenic poisoner Lyda Trueblood Dooley McHaffie Lewis Meyer Southard and the murders of her four dead husbands, a two-year-old daughter, and a brother-in-law. Lyda, like many other husband killers, murdered for insurance money. The tool of her trade? The arsenic she boiled off the flypaper.  Lyda made headlines as a femme fatale, a cold-blooded killer, and America’s Lady Bluebeard, and later, prison escapee.

Read: Hell’s Princess: The Mystery of Belle Gunness, Butcher of Men by Harold Schechter

If anything, Belle was direct. Very. She’d more likely smack a visitor over the head and feed them to the hogs on her Indiana Farm than use poison. She killed at least 14 people (including her husbands and children), though estimates go as high as 40 victims. Like Stella and Lyda, she did it for the money. She vanished when her farmhouse burned to the ground in 1908, spawning a mystery that has captivated true crime fans for more than a century. Did she get away with it?

Read: American Mother: The True Story of the Seattle Cyanide Killer by Gregg Olsen

Housewife and barfly, Stella Nickell had dreams bigger than her double-wide trailer. She concocted a scheme to kill her husband Bruce by reenacting the unsolved Chicago Tylenol cyanide poisonings. To make it appear a random act, she placed bottles of poison-laced headache capsules on grocery shelves, killing a mother of two, Sue Snow.

Former ambulance dispatcher Stacy Castor used antifreeze to kill two husbands. Michael was first, in 2000; his death was ruled a heart attack. Five years later, she staged husband David’s death as a suicide using a turkey baster as the implement to deliver the poison. Two years later, when suspicions turned into a bona fide investigation, Stacy tried to kill her daughter in order to keep her quiet. Not mom or wife of the year, for sure.

Nannie Doss takes the cake – at Halloween and in the annals of true crime. Indeed, the grandmother and frequent widow had a couple of those “ironic” nicknames – the “Giggling Granny” and the “Jolly Black Widow.” She seemed to enjoy the attention that her crimes brought. Nannie murdered at least 11 family members (four of whom were husbands) between 1927 and 1954 – making her America’s number one female murderer. No laughing matter by any stretch

Gregg Olsen is the #1 _New York Times_, #1 Amazon Charts, #1 _Wall Street Journal_, _Washington Post _and _USA Today_ bestselling author of more than 30 crime books. Based in rural Washington State, Olsen is a two-time Edgar Award-nominated author whose work has been honoured by the Mystery Writers of America and International Thriller Writers. His career has reached more than 8 million readers worldwide across print, ebook, audio, and Kindle Unlimited, including his breakout _hit If You Tell_, which spent more than 260 weeks in the Amazon Top 20.

7 essential 1970s NYC noir books and films

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Noir fiction has been defined as a world of cynicism, fatalism, and moral ambiguity. Although it may have originated between the two World Wars in Dashiell Hammett’s San Francisco and Raymond Chandler’s Los Angeles, noir was reborn in the 1970’s of New York City. Each decade has its own quality, reflecting the social and political turmoil of its time, but the NYC 1970’s remains distinctive for the gritty style of the books and movies instilled from real life.

All of these stories have the personal struggles, the morally ambiguous decisions, and the political cynicism that form noir’s fatalism. Each novel ends with satisfying operatic tragedy, the dark and the light that coexists in the chiaroscuro world of noir. My teenage years spanned the 1970’s in New York City, and my latest novel The Spiral is a noir thriller set in that time and place with a heavy dose of memoir.

This novel is told through a fictional police report that slowly builds the fascinating account. The NYPD hired a wave of unconventional recruits meant to help the police department modernize. A young detective, Bo Lockley, assigned to the Times Square area, struggles with his idealism while working with the cynical veterans. He cannot stop the crime and destruction that occur around him, he is too innocent and the world is too dark.

But his occasional influence on the people around him, and the world’s impact on him, is the heart of the narrative. Both the book and the film carry the weight of the time. The film, directed by Milton Katselas and written by Abby Mann and Ernest Tidyman, has an outstanding cast, most notably Michael Moriarty and Yaphet Kotto, and the well-crafted script respects the original work.

Told from more than one perspective, Across 110th Street presents tragedies with realism, and emotional and sociopolitical depth. The robbery of an organized crime payroll puts the robbers, the police detectives, and the criminal drug lords in a triangle of pressure. In typical noir fashion, there is no way out, but the change that happens to the characters, as the story unfolds, gives the satisfying impact.

The compelling book reads like important historical testimony. Unfortunately, it is quite difficult to find a copy. The film, directed by Barry Shear and written by Luther Davis, is well made, magnificently portrayed by Yaphet Kotto and Anthony Quinn, extremely well-paced, and has an exceptional soundtrack.

Peter Maas, Frank Serpico, Serpico: The Classic Story of the Cop Who Couldn’t Be Bought

This nonfiction book is a critical account of the depth and complexity of corruption in the system of law and order from the time. It is an engaging biography of Frank Serpico whose background and personal history growing up in New York is an important precursor to the events that lead to the near tragic end.

While this story may only be considered on the fringe of noir, the historical background is essential reading to understand NYC noir fiction of the time. The film with Al Pacino as Serpico, directed by Sidney Lumet and written by Waldo Salt and Norman Wexler, is a superb visual representation, and should not be missed.

This novel was inspired by true events and starts at the very beginning of the life story of Theresa from the Bronx, a woman who has been on a downhill journey since childhood. Her life is articulated in a way that pulls us right alongside her, through her unlucky life events, misguided experiences, poor decision-making, and damaging self-treatment, all giving a realistic understanding of what it was like to live in that era. It is the full backstory of a dedicated teacher and lonely lost soul, in the film played brilliantly by Diane Keaton, as she consistently puts herself in dangerous situations that finally lead to the tragic ending.

Adapted for the screen and directed by Richard Brooks, he gives the story a thriller angle with a slightly different focus. Read the book first, and Theresa’s story will resonate even more forcefully.

Working class eighteen-year-old Stony is struggling to find himself in the macho construction worker world of his father and uncle, while trying to navigate the mental illness of his mother and little brother. In the dark confusion of his life, the social pressures, the family drama, he finds a glimmer of light when he discovers he has a talent and compassion for working with kids. But will the circumstances that surround him allow him to live the life he wants to live?

This 1970s noir portrait of a young man is beautifully drawn in the book, bringing us along with him on his rollercoaster journey. In the film, skillfully directed by Robert Mulligan and adapted for the screen by Walter Newman, Richard Gere gives an inspired performance as Stony and shares the screen with an excellent supporting cast.

Vincent Patrick, The Pope of Greenwich Village

Distant cousins Charlie and Paulie, living on the edge of organized crime, are two men who wouldn’t recognize a good decision if one fell on their heads. The interior lives of these two characters makes this book engaging as they struggle with their life choices forming a story that is more than a cautionary tale.

The film, directed by Stuart Rosenberg and adapted by the author, is more polished than many of its gritty counterparts, but it is worth watching, most especially for the performances of Mickey Roarke and Eric Roberts in their sparkling youth.

Lawrence Block, The Sins of the Fathers (Matthew Scudder Series #1)

The details and descriptions in Lawrence Block’s Matthew Scudder series are required reading when analyzing the 1970’s in New York City. Matthew Scudder is an ex-police detective, struggling with alcoholism and other demons. He’s no longer a cop, nor is he a licensed private investigator, but if someone asks nicely, and has cash to spend, he’ll take a case so he can pay the rent on his room at a flophouse and send money to his ex-wife and kids.

This first book is an interesting insight into a world of part-time sex workers and seedy lives on the fringe. The ending has a strange twist, but it is the haunting details of the lives of so many sad and lost individuals that make this story, and the entire series, essential reading for 70s noir exploration. Follow Matthew Scudder from the first to the last of the books in the series and watch him and New York City evolve.

Sadly, there is no adaptation for the screen of this novel. A few other books in the Matthew Scudder series were adapted for the screen but none of them retain the exquisite NYC 70s aspects so integral to the series.

Rosanne Limoncelli is an author, filmmaker, and storyteller living in Brooklyn. She has written, directed, and produced short narrative films, documentaries, and educational films. Rosanne also writes plays, screenplays, poetry, games, mysteries, and science fiction. Her short fiction has appeared in Alfred Hitchcock Mystery Magazine, Suspense Magazine and Noir Nation, and her short films have been screened in festivals around the world. The Four Queens of Crime is her debut mystery novel. Dr. Limoncelli has taught writing and filmmaking for more than three decades and is the Senior Director for Film Technologies at the Kanbar Institute and the Martin Scorsese Virtual Production Center at NYU’s Tisch School of the Arts.

Acornia, once a prosperous tree village, now stands in ruins due to the Duramen’s influence – a dark mirror dimension teeming with angry spirits.

Right, I confess. I have an annoying new hobby. While record collecting has been the epitome of millennial hipster cool for the past decade, it's become a little too pricey for my liking, which is what sparked my interest in its arguably less sexy cousin, CD collecting.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The former Liverpool left-back had a couple of high-profile fallings-out in his time

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Trouble had a way of finding John Arne Riise during his playing days.

He infamously had a run-in with John Carew while on international duty with Norway in 2003, with the Liverpool left-back reportedly getting sparked out by the big striker following an argument Riise started by spitting at Carew's feet.

Then, in February 2007, a ridiculous argument over karaoke led Craig Bellamy to set about Riise with a golf club as the Norwegian lay in his hotel bed after a 'team bonding trip' to Portugal.

Riise reflected to FourFourTwo: "The thing with Carew only happened quickly, and we spoke the same night.

"It shouldn't have happened, but we talked about it the same day and have been friends ever since.

"The thing with Bellamy was more threatening because it was with a golf club.

It sounds stupid, and people think I'm kidding, but he went for my shins. It could have ended my career.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

"He was drunk and all over the place mentally, so that was scarier. If I didn't jump out of bed and protect my shins, I would have been done.

"But at the same time, if I met those two one against one, I'd be more scared of Carew than of Bellamy, for sure."

Amusingly, Bellamy and Riise both scored for Liverpool as they beat Barcelona to progress on their way to the 2007 final - and even more bizarrely, that incident with Bellamy went on to play a part in Wrexham's history, of all clubs. No pun intended.

As reported at the time, Bellamy requested that part of his fine should be donated to ailing Wrexham, who were then second-bottom of League Two.

A Wrexham statement later said: "The directors of Wrexham FC would personally like to say a very big thank you on behalf of all our supporters to Wales' own Craig Bellamy for the very generous donation he has made to Wrexham Football Club.

"This has allowed us to bring in three loan players to aid our fight for Football League survival."

Those loan signings Richard Walker (from Port Vale), Michael Proctor (from Hartlepool) and Tony Williams (from Carlisle), ultimately helped Wrexham rise well clear of the drop... though they were ultimately relegated the following season.

The Best Solar Gutter Lights of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 9:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Whether it’s for security or style, investing in solar gutter lights is an affordable way to upgrade any home. Though they aren’t quite as easy to install as solar lanterns, most solar gutter lights are generally easy to put up, typically attaching via clips to a home’s gutters. They can be installed as temporary or permanent fixtures that convert the day’s sun into attractive lighting on the exterior of a home. Many lights also include additional mounting hardware so they can be used above doorways, along fence posts, and as deck lights, making them both solar security lights and architectural features on a home’s exterior.

We recently hands-on tested five sets of solar gutter lights against 2 weeks of wind, rain, and plenty of sunshine to see how they held up. Though there were a number of winners, the UniqueFire Outdoor Solar Gutter Lights are our favorites. We like them for their durable construction, attractive design, and affordable price point.

While the price of solar gutter lights can be a factor, appearance and durability take center stage. Since they’ll be placed on the exterior of the home, it’s important to choose a style that matches a home’s aesthetic. And because these lights are frequently installed at high points on a home, low maintenance is key; no one wants to climb on a roof or up a ladder if they don’t have to. We took all this into account during our tests, and the result is the following list of the best solar gutter lights for the job.

Bottom line: Solar gutter lights offer an easy, cost-effective way to enhance home security and style. UniqueFire stands out as the best overall choice.

The following products are some of the best solar gutter lights on the market, including the five we tested for durability, light quality, installation, and water resistance. We’ve highlighted our favorite features below and included three additional picks we think are worth checking out for shoppers looking for the best solar gutter lights.

Our Ratings: Setup 5/5; Appearance 5/5; Light Quality 5/5; Durability 5/5; Value 5/5

With its easy-to-install bracket system, this set of six solar gutter lights was up on our house in just minutes. Once the lights were installed, we noticed that the added design element gave them an upgraded feel, despite their affordability. Because the lights can be purchased in either black or white, they can blend in with a wide range of home styles (although some buyers may prefer a rounded or smaller light). The lights come on automatically once it’s dark out and shut off before sunrise.

The lights themselves give off plenty of light, and we found the rectangle style to be more appealing if used on a fence post or railing. The solar-powered gutter lights stood up well to the elements and kept on shining throughout our test period. Because the manufacturer recommends replacing the rechargeable AA battery after 8 to 12 months, this may be an important detail for some buyers to factor in.

What our tester says: “Overall, we found these lights easy to put up, attractive, and at such a great price that we gave them the top spot on our list.”—Amber Guetebier, Product Reviews tester and writer

Falove’s outdoor solar gutter lights are available in a pack of eight, allowing users to set up an entire light scheme with one kit at a very reasonable price. They take just a few minutes to set up, but there’s one small issue: One of the included screws is a very small thumb screw and requires an equally small screwdriver to put in place. We ended up using an eyeglass kit to tighten it. While the process was straightforward, it was a little tedious, and installing several of these could get old fast.

Some LED gutter lights can seem too bright in a garden or backyard setting, but each of these lights is just 100 lumens, which illuminated the space nicely and gave the house an atmospheric glow. This set has a simple appearance, but it will blend in with most home styles. The bracket has two mounting options: one with the tiny screw that can be easily clipped onto the gutter lip, or a more permanent mounting onto a fence or other structure (this method comes with a much longer standard-size screw). Overall, these are pleasing lights, and they come at a very affordable price point. Like others, they came on once it was dark and turned off before sunrise.

Get the Falove solar gutter lights at Amazon.

The InnoGear solar gutter lights come in a pack of two. Since each light produces 450 lumens, these are bright lights worth checking out. The lights feature several modes, including low output, medium output, motion sensor, and full brightness, with a few combinations of these as well. For example, you can keep it on low mode combined with motion detection, and the light automatically brightens to full mode when it detects movement. For this reason, these are an excellent option for solar-powered security lights. They switch on automatically once it’s dark and switch back off at dawn.

The lights mount directly to the structure of a home. However, although these are advertised as solar gutter lights, they are not able to clip directly onto gutters like most gutter lights. They can be mounted either flush against the home like a sconce or with the included bar, which allows for positioning beyond eaves to ensure maximum light absorption. Because they look a little like miniature solar panels, they have a modern feel that may not appeal to everyone’s sense of style. Overall, these are high-quality outdoor solar security lights that passed all of the environmental tests with flying colors.

For some high-powered accent lighting around the home, shoppers can check out the DBF solar gutter lights. The lights in this six-pack use a two-way bracket that can clamp to gutters with the included thumb screw or screw directly into the structure of a fence or home. Each light produces up to 100 lumens and can run for more than 10 hours, coming on when it gets dark and shutting off closer to sunrise. We tested the black color with cool white light, but they are also available in white and with a warm white light.

Because they are similar to other solar gutter lights that clamp in place, we were able to install them without too much trouble. Though these are a little pricier than we think they ought to be, they are frequently marked down on Amazon, making them a better buy for the money than they may seem at first glance. Their unique UFO-like shape puts off a vaguely midcentury modern vibe, which may be appealing to some shoppers.

However, they are definitely less bright, and we can’t swear by their weather resistance, especially because no IP rating is provided, which indicates how well an electrical product stands up to water and dust.
