Why Businesses Should Validate AI Use Cases Before Investing in Development

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Artificial intelligence is becoming part of everyday business planning. Companies are exploring ways to automate repetitive tasks, improve customer service, analyze information, and reduce operational costs.

But there is an important difference between finding an interesting AI idea and identifying a problem that AI can actually solve.

Many businesses start discussing development before they fully understand what they want to improve. They select a technology, estimate a budget, and begin building a solution without checking whether the idea makes business sense.

This can lead to expensive projects that work technically but deliver little practical value.

Before spending money on AI development, businesses need to validate whether the proposed use case solves a real problem, has the right data, and can produce measurable results.

Taking time to answer these questions can prevent unnecessary spending and help companies focus on opportunities worth developing.

Every useful AI project should begin with a problem that employees or customers already experience.

A company might say it wants an AI chatbot because competitors are using one. But that does not explain what the chatbot needs to accomplish.

Are customers waiting too long for answers? Are support employees repeatedly handling the same questions? Are important requests being missed outside business hours?

Each problem requires a different approach.

For example, if customers cannot find basic order information, improving the existing customer portal may solve the issue without developing an AI assistant.

However, if support employees spend several hours answering varied questions across multiple systems, an AI solution could be worth exploring.

The first step is to understand the current process, identify where difficulties occur, and determine why they happen.

Without this understanding, businesses risk investing in technology that does not address the original problem.

Not every business activity needs artificial intelligence.

Some tasks follow simple rules and can be handled using traditional software automation. Others involve understanding documents, recognizing patterns, or working with large amounts of information.

The difference matters when deciding where to invest.

Consider a finance department that receives hundreds of invoices every week.

If employees simply copy information between two compatible systems, a standard integration may be enough.

But if invoices arrive in different formats and employees must read, classify, and extract information before entering it into accounting software, AI-powered document processing may offer greater value.

The business should examine how often the task occurs, how much time it requires, and what difficulties employees face.

Good opportunities are often found in processes involving repeated manual reviews, large document volumes, or information that takes too long to organize.

The objective is not to introduce AI everywhere. It is to find situations where its capabilities provide a meaningful advantage.

A problem can be frustrating without being expensive or important enough to justify a new AI solution.

For example, employees might spend ten minutes each week preparing a small report. Automating that task could save time, but the development and maintenance costs may exceed the benefit.

Compare this with a logistics company where employees spend several hours every day checking shipment documents.

Delays in this process could affect customer communication, billing, and delivery schedules.

Improving that workflow may provide a stronger business case.

Companies should consider how frequently the problem occurs, how many people it affects, and what happens when the process is delayed or completed incorrectly.

They should also examine whether the problem affects revenue, operating costs, customer satisfaction, or compliance.

A useful AI use case should address a problem that matters to business performance, not simply an inconvenience that technology could remove.

An AI solution cannot perform reliably without access to suitable information.

Businesses sometimes assume that because they have years of customer records, invoices, or operational reports, their data is ready for AI development.

Information may be stored across multiple applications, contain missing fields, or follow inconsistent formats.

For example, a company planning an AI sales forecasting system may discover that customer records are incomplete and sales reports are updated differently across departments.

These issues can affect the quality of forecasts, even when the underlying technology works correctly.

Before approving development, teams need to confirm where the required data exists, whether it is accurate, and how it can be accessed.

They must also understand whether privacy or security requirements restrict its use.

Sometimes, improving data quality is the most valuable first step. Starting development before completing that work may lead to delays and additional costs.

A promising use case may still be difficult to implement if it does not fit into the company’s existing operations.

Businesses depend on ERP platforms, CRM tools, accounting systems, and other applications that employees use every day.

Introducing AI often means connecting these systems and changing how information moves between teams.

Imagine a company developing an AI tool to process purchase orders.

The tool may successfully extract order information, but employees still need a way to review exceptions, approve transactions, and update the ERP system.

If these steps are not planned, the solution may create another disconnected process.

Before committing to development, businesses should identify which systems AI needs to access, what information it must exchange, and where employee approval is required.

A use case becomes more practical when the complete workflow is understood, not just the individual AI task.

AI development requires more than an initial software budget.

Companies may need to pay for integration, data preparation, testing, employee training, security reviews, and ongoing maintenance.

These expenses should be compared with realistic business benefits.

For example, suppose a team spends 200 hours each month reviewing documents. If an AI solution could reduce the manual workload by 80 hours, the company can estimate the value of that time.

However, saved hours do not automatically become financial savings. The business must consider whether employees can use the time for other valuable work or whether costs will actually decrease.

It is also important to include the time employees will spend reviewing incorrect outputs and handling exceptions.

Businesses developing a wider adoption plan can use an enterprise AI roadmap focused on use cases and ROI to understand how business value, implementation readiness, and risk can guide investment decisions.

A realistic business case helps decision-makers compare opportunities instead of approving projects based on assumptions.

One of the most effective ways to validate an AI use case is to test it before committing to full development.

A pilot allows businesses to examine whether the proposed solution can handle real tasks using a limited amount of data and a controlled workflow.

For example, a company planning an AI document processing system might begin with one invoice type from a small group of suppliers.

The team can measure extraction accuracy, processing time, and the number of documents requiring manual correction.

This provides practical evidence about what works and what needs improvement.

A pilot should also test difficult situations, such as incomplete documents, unusual formats, and missing information.

The aim is not to create a perfect demonstration. It is to understand whether the solution can perform reliably under normal business conditions.

If the pilot reveals serious problems, the company can adjust its approach before spending more money.

Some AI applications support employees by preparing information or suggesting actions. Others may influence decisions involving customers, finances, or sensitive records.

These use cases require different levels of control.

For example, using AI to summarize internal meeting notes carries different risks from using it to approve financial transactions.

Before development begins, businesses should examine what could happen if the system produces an incorrect result.

Could it create financial losses? Could customers receive misleading information? Could confidential data become exposed?

Teams should decide where human review is necessary and how mistakes will be corrected.

They should also confirm who will be responsible for monitoring the system after deployment.

Identifying these risks early makes it easier to plan appropriate safeguards instead of adding them after problems occur.

Businesses often have several possible AI opportunities across sales, finance, operations, and customer support.

Choosing the first idea discussed in a meeting may mean overlooking a simpler project with greater potential.

A better approach is to compare opportunities using the same criteria.

Each proposed use case can be reviewed against five practical questions:

This comparison helps businesses identify opportunities that offer useful benefits without unnecessary complexity.

A smaller project with reliable data and clear results may be a better starting point than a large initiative involving several departments.

The strongest use case is not always the most ambitious one. It is the one the organization can realistically implement, manage, and improve.

Validating a use case does not always lead to approval.

Sometimes, the right decision is to delay development until the business has better data, stronger processes, or clearer objectives.

In other situations, traditional software may solve the problem at a lower cost.

Rejecting an AI idea does not mean the company is falling behind.

It means the organization is making a decision based on business needs rather than pressure to adopt new technology.

A useful validation process should allow teams to proceed, revise the idea, postpone it, or stop it completely.

This flexibility protects resources and leaves room for stronger opportunities.

AI can help businesses reduce repetitive work, improve access to information, and support better decisions. But those benefits are not guaranteed simply because a company invests in development.

The results depend heavily on choosing the right problem.

Before approving an AI project, businesses should understand the existing workflow, check data readiness, estimate potential value, review implementation challenges, and test important assumptions.

They should also be willing to explore simpler alternatives when AI is not necessary.

The purpose of validating AI use cases is not to slow down innovation. It is to make sure time, money, and effort are invested in solutions that can deliver genuine business value.

Companies that make these decisions early are better prepared to develop practical AI solutions that employees can use and the business can sustain.

Shashank Jaiswal is the Co-Founder and Chief Information Officer at SDLC Corp, with more than 10 years of experience in enterprise technology consulting, solution architecture, and software implementation. His work covers ERP and CRM systems, SaaS development, workflow automation, and custom enterprise software. He focuses on aligning scalable technology architectures with business requirements and helping organizations modernize their enterprise systems.

Singapore Airlines Unveils Posh New First Class Suites With Double Beds For 777-9 And A350ULR

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Singapore Airlines has unveiled its next-generation first class seat, with sliding doors, companion dining, and center seats that combine into a double bed measuring five feet across. The new product will appear on the Airbus A350-900ULR and Boeing 777-9, bringing first class to the airline’s ultra-long-range A350 fleet for the first time.

I also wrote about the carrier’s new business class product here.

Singapore Airlines’ new first class specifications reveal a small cabin with substantial personal space. The A350-900ULR will have four seats, while the Boeing 777-9 will have six. Both use a 1-2-1 arrangement with direct aisle access and a curved cabin layout.

PriestmanGoode designed the product, with Collins Aerospace manufacturing the seats. The interiors feature Poltrona Frau leather and padded walls incorporating Singapore Airlines’ batik motif.

The centerpiece is the pair of center seats. A fully retractable divider allows passengers to share the space, with the seats moving together to create either a two-person sofa or a double bed. That bed measures 80 inches long and 60 inches wide. Singapore Airlines describes it as the largest double bed in commercial aviation.

Solo passengers receive an 80-inch-long, 30-inch-wide bed. The seat itself measures 23 inches across with the armrests raised, expanding to 30 inches when they are lowered.

Every seat also supports face-to-face dining with a companion, a feature I appreciate in first class: traveling together should include the option to sit across from one another for dinner, even when you prefer separate window seats.

The seat includes electronically adjustable side wings, upper and lower lumbar support, adjustable armrests, a leg rest, and an ottoman. Integrated myKrisWorld controls can save preferred seat positions and lighting settings for future flights.

For sleeping, the crew will prepare the bed with cotton linens, a duvet, a choice of pillows, and a new mattress topper from German bedding company Paradies. Singapore Airlines says the topper uses breathable spacer fabric to improve cushioning and temperature regulation.

Dedicated storage includes a personal wardrobe, a leather-lined vanity drawer, and space for cabin baggage beneath the ottoman.

Both aircraft receive a 32-inch 4K entertainment screen, but the A350ULR version is OLED. The 777-9 specification lists a conventional 4K display.

Passengers will be offered Bang & Olufsen wireless headphones or can pair Bluetooth headphones with the system. A separate touch panel displays flight information, connectivity status, and lavatory availability without interrupting the main screen.

The new cabins are scheduled to begin entering service in the second half of 2027. Unlike with ANA, the unveiling does not signal an imminent onboard debut. First class is planned for the A350-900ULR and 777-9. The standard long-haul A350-900 will receive the new business class, but no first class cabin.

Singapore Airlines’ new first class looks lovely, particularly for couples who can use the center seats as a shared living and sleeping space. Companion dining at every seat and substantial bedding are welcome details too.

Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

At least 10 local governments have moved to drop Flock’s surveillance cameras since summer

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

At least 10 local governments and police departments have moved since this summer to curb, restrict, or end their use of Flock Safety technology as concerns about mass surveillance and data privacy continue to grow. The backlash is part of a broader pushback against the rapid adoption of advanced AI technology, including the data center buildout, facial recognition, and other uses of AI.

Flock, founded in 2017, sells surveillance software — like license plate readers, drones, and microphone-based edge-computing devices to pick up gunshots and other sounds — that can help solve crimes, such as finding missing people or stolen cars. More than 100,000 Flock cameras are in use nationwide.

But in the past three months, Monroe, Ohio; Newburgh, New York; Richmond, Virginia; Hopkinton, Massachusetts; New Bedford, Massachusetts; and Lansing, Michigan, have all moved to end their contracts with Flock. Meanwhile, police in East Bridgewater and Grafton, Massachusetts, also announced plans to stop using Flock cameras. Just this week, residents of League City, Texas, voted against their police department’s continued use of Flock’s license plate readers, and the cameras are now set to be removed.

The concerns range from privacy to how data is shared (and with whom) to procurement concerns.

In Asheville, North Carolina, police lost access to Flock after the city voted to terminate its contract in August, and the El Paso County Sheriff’s Office in Colorado announced in September that it would stop using Flock cameras. When asked for comment, the El Paso County Sheriff’s Office redirected TechCrunch to its press release, where Sheriff Joseph Roybal said that discontinuing the use of Flock was the right decision for the county.

“We have the tools, training, and experience to locate and apprehend criminals without relying on Flock cameras,” he said in the press release.

Tatiana Rice, a senior director for U.S. legislation at The Future of Privacy Forum, told TechCrunch that the backlash to Flock isn’t surprising and only further represents the “growing public skepticism toward technology.” Flock is a target because people can see the sensors and cameras as they drive along roads; data centers have also become physical embodiments of the resentment growing against emerging technology. “A camera at the end of your street is tangible in a way that everyday sharing and selling of personal data online is not,” Rice said.

At the state level this summer, for example, Florida barred license plate readers from state highways, while Texas paused state funding for Flock. The issues again range from data privacy concerns to misuse of the technology.

Misuse has been a real problem. In August, for example, an officer in Brentwood, Missouri was accused of using Flock cameras to stalk his ex-wife during their divorce. In September, an officer from East Texas was sentenced to prison for using Flock to stalk his ex-girlfriend and her family.

Another growing concern is how immigration officials have been able to access the technology. One case that captures the issue came in March, when Ithaca, New York, passed a resolution to terminate its contract with the Flock Group.

In the resolution, the city said there were “proven instances of Flock Safety infrastructure being used by federal agents for information purposes even without specific authorizations from the contracting locality,” and that the city had reviewed reports that “either intentionally or through its negligence,” Flock had allowed U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives (ATF) to “use query results from its database of state and local ALPR [automatic license plate reader] data to investigate and persecute immigrants throughout the United States.”

“Every day, police departments across the country are using Flock technology to solve serious crimes, locate suspects, find missing people, and make a measurable impact on public safety,” Flock told TechCrunch in a statement. “While we respect every community’s decision, removing a tool that is actively helping solve violent crimes and protect communities moves public safety backward. Every Flock customer owns its data and controls who it is shared with and how it can be used. Flock does not sell data and does not have a contract with ICE.”

This August, Flock announced it would shorten the default period it stores data for new customers, from 30 days to seven. It also said agencies now need a case code before they can search the database.

The conversation around Flock echoes, in many ways, the fight over data centers. Many of these cities adopted Flock just a few years ago, and residents likely had no idea it was being installed, Adam Schwartz of the Electronic Frontier Foundation, a digital privacy nonprofit that has investigated Flock before, told TechCrunch.

That lack of awareness is similar to how data centers are popping up across the country, often before residents understand what is happening. Schwartz said police departments often decide to use Flock on their own, with little oversight from city councils. In South Windsor, Connecticut, according to local reports, Flock cameras went live last year without public warning or a vote. In October, the town voted to remove its Flock cameras.

“Grassroots activists across the country have been demanding that city councils exercise their procurement power to refuse to pay for ALPRs,” Schwartz said, referring to councils’ power to decide what the city buys. He added that activists have been fighting for years and that “many city councils are listening.”

The main argument for technology like Flock is that it helps combat crime and keep neighborhoods safe. And many local governments are not dropping Flock or similar technology; instead, they are adding restrictions on its use or looking for alternatives.

Cleveland, Ohio, for example, voted this summer to extend its contract with Flock, but has started restricting searches related to immigration. It has also limited access to the database to the Cleveland Police alone, cutting off the Northeast Ohio Regional Fusion Center, essentially a federal intelligence agency, which had initially been given access.

“They are used and needed for investigative purposes,” the city said when reached for comment. “The Department of Public Safety is currently reviewing responses to an RFP [request for proposals] that was sent out to other vendors that provide the same technology as Flock.”

But as with data centers, communities want more say in the trade-offs. “These tools can offer real public safety benefits, but communities are clearly interested in ensuring that those benefits are not at the cost of their civil liberties,” Rice said. Surveillance technology will likely remain a political hot topic, especially since Democrats now control Congress.

In September, President Donald Trump said he liked Flock’s technology “because of law enforcement,” even though a Reuters poll that same month found that more Americans dislike the technology than like it.

Also that month, Rep. Thomas Massie, a Republican from Kentucky, introduced a bill called the Flock-Off Act to bar the use of federal money to buy “Flock-style surveillance” and put other safeguards in place. In October, Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.), Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-N.Y.), and Sen. Jeff Merkley (D-Ore.) introduced the Ban Flock Act to end “the rise of AI-powered surveillance cameras.”
